Probed as a genuine Reform member (control `role=authenticated  sub set=t  is_manager_up=f  is_service_context=f`) against a **real occurrence** and a **real throwaway period created in the same transaction** — so the guard fires rather than the PT404 "no such period" path that hosted's zero periods would otherwise hit — the whole thing ending in `raise`: both returned **PT403** before writing, and hosted still shows **0 periods / 0 records / 0 settlements** afterward. Neither has a per-instructor arm: `record_class_pay` is `is_manager_up OR is_service_context`, `record_pay_settlement` is `is_manager_up` alone, so a non-subject instructor is refused by the identical branch that refused the member. **`instructor_week`'s AND `pay_statement`'s per-instructor arms ARE NOW PROVEN ON HOSTED.** A real instructor login exists (`drewdeanna15@gmail.com` → instructor `2202d1d7`, invited+claimed through the product — see the 149–156 note). Probed as that instructor (control `auth_instructor_id=2202d1d7  is_manager_up=false  is_service_context=false`, block ending in `raise`): **`instructor_week(another instructor)` refused PT403 and `instructor_week(self)` RETURNED**. `pay_statement` was OWED for a data reason — it looks the period up FIRST, so with 0 hosted periods a dummy id returned PT404 before the guard (the "a guard that never fires looks like a guard that passes" trap). **That is now closed:** Reform's payroll contract was entered through the UI (semimonthly, second-day 16, settle offset 0, ₱900/₱75/2/₱1,400, conversion ₱500/30d) and its first pay period was created through `ensure_pay_period` (`3571dc6c…`, 2026-09-16→30). Probed with that real period as instructor `2202d1d7`: **`pay_statement(another instructor)` refused PT403, `pay_statement(self)` returned `ok=true` with `settle_on=2026-09-30`** — the offset-0 pay date (= `ends_on`), so Decision 31's settle date flows through `pay_statement` on hosted too. The guard is also proven locally in `instructor_portal_test.sql`. **EVERYTHING THROUGH 133 IS APPLIED ON HOSTED.** The advisor was run for 133: `member_bootstrap` came back `anon=false authed=true` (the drop+recreate is authenticated-only, not anon), the folded `guest_passes_enabled`/`has_payment_provider` columns are in the hosted signature, and the anon surface is still exactly the nine pre-login functions. **EVERYTHING THROUGH 132 IS APPLIED ON HOSTED** — verified with `supabase migration list --linked`, `20260831360000` (131) and `20260831370000` (132) both carry remote timestamps. **THE ADVISOR QUERY HAS BEEN RUN AGAINST HOSTED FOR 131 AND 132, both halves.** Grant half: the anon surface is still exactly the nine pre-login functions; `studio_member_settings` survived its drop+recreate as `authed`-only (NOT anon); `member_milestones` and all nine announcement functions are `authed`-only; the `announcements`/`announcement_dismissals` tables have RLS on. Guard half, probed as a genuine Reform member (control `role=authenticated  auth.uid set=t  is_manager_up=f  is_service_context=f`, ending in `raise` so nothing committed): `staff_announcements` and `instructor_announcements` refused a member with **PT403** (manager-up / staff guards), `member_announcements` and `member_milestones` refused a studio the member is not in with **PT403** while returning normally for their own studio. `scripts/check-null-guards.py` reports **IN STEP** — 132's readers use the null-safe idiom (`select … into v_member … where user_id = auth.uid()` then `if v_member is null then raise`), not a bare equality. (130 was IN STEP at 342, `cancel_booking` re-probed as a real member and it refuses PT403.) (127 was believed pushed once before it actually was; the list is the authority.) **THE ADVISOR QUERY HAS BEEN RUN AGAINST HOSTED FOR 127 AND 128, both halves.** Grant half: the anon surface is still exactly the nine pre-login functions; `book_guest`, `guest_pass_eligibility`, `sign_waiver`, `guest_pass_report`, `dashboard_guest_kpi` are `authed`-only; `studio_member_settings` survived its drop+recreate as `authed`-only (NOT anon); the triggers `tg_guest_pass_sync`/`enforce_guest_waiver` and the internal `occurrence_seats_held` are closed to every client role; and 128's `sweep_guest_waivers` is service-role only with its cron `studiior-guest-waivers :: */15 * * * *` present. Guard half, probed as a genuine Reform member (control `role=authenticated  auth.uid set=t  is_manager_up=f  is_service_context=f`, ending in `raise` so nothing committed): `guest_pass_report` and `dashboard_guest_kpi` refused with **PT403**; but **`sign_waiver` against another member returned NOT REFUSED** (the null-guard hole) and **`guest_pass_eligibility` ran with no auth check** — the two things migration 129 closes. 

After 111, which dropped and recreated `studio_by_slug()`, the advisor query against hosted returned exactly the nine pre-login surfaces. **The advisor query has now been run against hosted for 122–126, both halves.** It was owed lightly: 122 adds a column only; 123 and 124 re-issue existing functions with `create or replace` (which keeps their ACL) and add no new client-callable functions; 125 adds three functions all closed to every client role (`sweep_waitlist`, `offer_waitlist_seat`, `queue_waitlist_missed` — service-role only) plus a `create or replace` of `notification_wanted`; and 126 re-issues `book_class` with `create or replace` (keeps its ACL), adds `occurrence_seats_held` closed to every client role, and adds `occurrence_holds` — the one new `authenticated`-callable surface, member-guarded, the migration-056 shape. **Confirmed on hosted:** grant half — the anon surface is still exactly the nine pre-login functions; `book_class` is `anon=false authed=true`, so its ACL survived the `create or replace` re-issue; `occurrence_holds` is `authed` only; `occurrence_seats_held` (126) and `sweep_waitlist`/`offer_waitlist_seat`/`queue_waitlist_missed` (125) are reachable by neither client role. Guard half — probed as a genuine Reform Collective member (control line `role=authenticated  auth.uid set=t  is_manager_up=f  is_service_context=f`, the probe ending in `raise` so nothing committed): `occurrence_holds` returned normally for the member's own studio and refused a studio they are not a member of with **PT403 “you are not a member of that studio”**, so the guard fires before returning any data.

**THE ADVISOR QUERY HAS BEEN RUN AGAINST HOSTED FOR 117–121 (challenges), both halves.** Grant half: all 23 challenge functions are closed to `anon`. The eleven a client can reach — `create_challenge`, `update_challenge`, `publish_challenge`, `staff_challenges`, `challenge_overview`, `member_challenges`, `member_challenge_detail`, `join_challenge`, `dashboard_challenge_kpi`, `dashboard_absent_cards`, and `auth_participates_in` (called inside the `cp_leaderboard` RLS policy, so it needs `authenticated`) — guard inside. The twelve internals, triggers and the sweep — `challenge_qualifies`, `recompute_participant`, `rank_challenge`, `challenge_goal_line`, the five `queue_challenge_*`, `sweep_challenges`, `tg_challenge_progress` — are reachable by neither client role. **The drop/recreate of `dashboard_absent_cards` (authed) and `notification_wanted` (closed to both) did NOT reopen either to `anon`.** The anon surface is still exactly nine.

**Guard half — `member_challenge_detail` is the migration-056 shape (takes an id, returns another member's leaderboard) and was probed as a genuine member.** Hosted has one studio and one member, so the probe inserted a temporary login-less co-participant and a challenge, read as the real member, and ended in `raise` so the whole transaction was discarded — nothing persisted on production. Control line `role=authenticated  auth.uid set=t  is_manager_up=f  is_service_context=f`. **Board OFF:** `member_challenge_detail.leaderboard` is null, the member's own `progress` comes back, and a direct read of `challenge_participants` returns **1 row — their own, nobody else's**; a member UPDATE of their own progress affects **0 rows** (the `cp_member_self` narrowing). **Board ON:** the leaderboard returns both rows — the member's own (`is_me true`) and the co-participant — and the direct read returns 2. Board off returning the member's own row and no other is the case that most needed proving, and it holds.

**THE ADVISOR QUERY HAS BEEN RUN AGAINST HOSTED FOR 114–116, both halves.** The grant half is clean: `anon` reaches **exactly the nine** pre-login surfaces and nothing else. **116 dropped and recreated `schedule_range()` and it did NOT come back anon-callable** — the specific worry, since a dropped function is reborn with hosted's default `anon`/`authenticated` grants that no local reset shows, and this is the third time that function has been touched. The migration's own revoke ran there and held: `schedule_range` is `anon=false, authed=true`, guarded inside by `is_manager_up OR is_service_context`. 114's `open_shift` and 116's `evaluate_commitment` are `authed` and guarded; 114's `notify_open_shifts`, its `tg_stamp_shift_opened` trigger and 116's `tg_flex_reached_minimum` trigger are reachable by neither client role. The other half — the migration-056 shape, a SECURITY DEFINER function that takes an id and returns another person's tenant data — is `instructor_reliability()` from 115. **Probed as a genuine Reform Collective member** (control line `role=authenticated  auth.uid set=t  is_manager_up=f  is_service_context=f`), it refused with **PT403 "that is somebody else's record"** before reading anything, and the probe ended in `raise` so nothing committed. Its guard is `is_this_instructor OR is_manager_up`, and it fires first. 115's other authed functions — `withdraw_application`, `withdraw_from_shift`, `approve_shift_application` — all guard (`auth_instructor_id`/`is_manager_up`) before any write, confirmed by reading the migration.

**The previous revision of this paragraph said six migrations were unpushed and that was false.** 093, 094, 095, 096 and 097 were already on hosted; the claim was carried forward from a stale session note without running the list. That is this file's own "a stale warning is worse than no warning" in its most embarrassing form — a warning about unpushed migrations, itself unchecked. **Run `supabase migration list --linked` before repeating any claim about what is or is not on hosted.**

**THE ADVISOR QUERY HAS BEEN RUN AGAINST HOSTED FOR 089–109, both halves.**

**Decision 24's twelve functions, checked on hosted after 109 landed:** `anon` reaches **none** of them. The four closed to `authenticated` as well are the two triggers and the two sweeps — `tg_peak_allowance`, `tg_record_infraction`, `sweep_no_shows`, `sweep_peak_cutoff_reminders` — plus `occurrence_is_peak`, which is an internal the guarded readers call. Every one of the eight a client can reach guards before it does any work. The anon surface is still **exactly nine**.

The grant half is clean. `anon` reaches **exactly nine** surfaces and they are the right nine — `studio_by_slug`, `studio_invite_preview`, `accept_studio_invite`, `member_invite_preview`, `claim_member_account`, `instructor_invite_preview`, `claim_instructor_account`, `stripe_webhook`, `stripe_platform_webhook` — with no extras and none absent. **102's drop-and-recreate of `activate_purchase()` did NOT reopen it on hosted**, which was the specific worry: a dropped function is reborn with hosted's default `anon` and `authenticated` grants that no local reset can show. The migration's own revoke ran there and held, and only the eight-argument signature exists, so no seven-argument overload survived the drop. `plan_seats_taken` and `stripe_handle_checkout_completed` are closed to both client roles; `plan_seats` is `authenticated` alone and guarded inside.

**The other half found one fault.** Of the twenty-six SECURITY DEFINER functions 089–102 leave callable by `authenticated`, twenty-three guard before doing any work — spot-checked for the early-return trap that caught `claw_back_conversion_bonus()`, and `dashboard_facts`, `memberships_due`, `message_draft_for` and `advance_membership_period` all raise before they read anything. Two of the remaining three are `instructor_invite_preview` and `claim_instructor_account`, pre-login by nature, where the hashed single-use token IS the credential.

**The third was `membership_frozen_now(uuid)`, and migration 103 closes it.** It takes a membership id, steps over the RLS on `memberships`, and answers a boolean about it — the same class of leak as a row, which is why 086 closed `occurrence_is_adjacent()`, "a boolean about somebody else's day". It **lost the grant rather than gaining a guard**, 086's stronger answer: its only callers anywhere are `advance_membership_period()`, `memberships_due()` and `sweep_membership_periods()`, all of which have already checked. Migration 095 granted it to `authenticated` beside its own revoke, in the shape of a public reader, with nothing asking for it.

**`advance_membership_period()` is left client-callable on purpose and the reasoning is recorded in 103.** It is guarded desk-up and `manual_payments_test.sql` exercises it as a client deliberately — PT403 for a stranger, PT422 for a pack, PT409 for a frozen membership. Closing it would delete four real assertions to buy a within-tenant hardening nobody asked for. **Recorded, not acted on:** reachable on its own it lets front desk roll a membership forward with no payment row, and that row is the whole trace the manual path exists to leave.

**`ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` has to be set in Vault on hosted before any narrative is generated there.** Without it every row lands `failed` with a readable reason and the written sentences stand, which is the designed behaviour and not an outage — but it means the AI half is not live until somebody sets it.

**DECISION 24 SHIPS IN PIECES, AND SEAT CAPS ARE THE FIRST.** Peak allowance, daily booking caps, a suspension ladder and seat caps on a plan are **three independent switches, not one feature** — turning one on must not reveal the others. Seat caps are built; the other two are decided and not built. `docs/STUDIIOR_V1_DECISIONS.md` Decision 24 records the whole of it, including what is deliberately deferred.

**THE BRIEF NAMED A FUNCTION THAT HAS NEVER EXISTED.** `release_booking_entitlements()` is not in this codebase and never was. The seam it describes is real and is `tg_stamp_booking_release()` — a TRIGGER, from migration 079, whose body has carried a comment marking the exact spot since the day it was written. The difference matters for the work still to come: a booking is released from four places including a hand-written UPDATE, which is why the seam is a trigger, and the entitlement logic will be a new function that trigger calls rather than a branch in an existing one.

**THE FEATURE'S "OFF" IS A SWITCH, AND THE SUITE PROVES IT IS THE SWITCH.** `seat_caps_enabled` defaults false. The test studio with the switch OFF carries `max_active_members = 1` on its own plan and sells three memberships through it — so removing the switch check makes that studio start refusing, which is exactly what the teeth check shows. That is the flex lesson applied in advance: "a studio with flex off has nothing pending" once passed with the gate deleted, because that studio had no flex rows to gate. A third studio has nothing configured at all, and sees nothing.

**`plan_seats()` RETURNS NO ROWS when the switch is off — not zeroes, not nulls.** "This studio does not use places" and "this plan has no limit" are the same absence and neither renders anything. The plans list keeps reading "8 members" for every uncapped plan and grows no column of dashes.

**FROZEN KEEPS THE SEAT AND THE RATE, AND THAT IS WHAT FREEZING IS.** `plan_seats_taken()` counts `trialing`, `active`, `past_due` and `frozen`. A member who pauses for January and comes back to find her place sold and the price raised has been given a cancellation with extra steps. `past_due` counts for the same class of reason: somebody who owes money has not left, and §7.3's grace exists precisely for that. Cancelling frees the place, and §7.1's snapshot means the old rate leaves with her — asserted by raising the price and buying again.

**THE PLAN ROW IS LOCKED BEFORE THE COUNT.** `activate_purchase()` is the only thing that creates a membership, so the cap lives there and nowhere else; `select ... for update` makes the count and the insert one serialised step per plan. It is taken whether or not the plan is capped, because deciding whether to lock by reading the cap first is itself the read-then-write the lock exists to prevent. It is not a wall against a hand-written INSERT into `memberships`, which takes no lock — the same standing caveat as `booked_count`, and `import_commit()` inserts directly on purpose, because importing existing members is recording what is already true.

**A COMPLETED STRIPE CHECKOUT IS NEVER REFUSED.** The charge is captured by the time the event arrives, so raising would return a non-2xx, be retried for days, and leave somebody who has paid holding no membership. `activate_purchase()` gained `p_enforce_seat_cap` and the checkout handler passes `false`. The studio goes one over and every screen says "4 of 3 — over"; the desk is still refused while over, which is the whole point of being told. A PARAMETER rather than a transaction-local flag, because the caller is calling the function directly — the flags in this codebase exist where a trigger cannot see which path is running.

**Adding that parameter meant DROPPING the function**, since a new argument with a default creates an overload rather than replacing a signature and every seven-argument call then fails as ambiguous (migration 028's trap). A drop discards the ACL, so 102 re-asserts the grants and then **asserts them in the migration itself** (094's shape).

**Rebuilding `stripe_handle_checkout_completed` from memory produced a wrong function, and typing it out is what exposed that.** The reconstruction dropped a member-belongs-to-this-studio check entirely and reordered two declarations whose initialisers depend on each other. It was replaced with the exact text of migration 051 — the newest FILE that defines it — with one line changed. The rule was already in this file; this is what ignoring it looks like when you only need to change one argument.

**`on_limit_reached` is `hide` or `staff_only`, and `waitlist` is absent from the CHECK.** A waiting list for a place on a PLAN is a table, an offer with an expiry, a template, a staff screen and a promotion path — the class waitlist over again against a different scarce thing. Storing a choice nothing implements is the decorative control this build refuses to draw, so the constraint refuses it and the suite asserts the refusal.

**CORAL DOES NOT SET A SENTENCE, and I broke that rule in two places before measuring.** `#D9401A` is 4.47 on white and the floor is 4.5. The plans list rendered "7 of 5 places — over" in `text-coral` and the sell screen rendered the full-plan warning the same way; **both measured 4.47 in the rendered DOM**. Coral now carries the meaning as a tint and a rule with ink on it — the warning measures **15.08** — and the list's "over" is a chip rather than coloured type. Four screens re-measured afterwards: plans list, plan edit, settings and the sell screen, **0 elements below 4.5, lowest 4.59**, which is `--ink-3` on white and the documented floor.

**A DATE-DEPENDENT UNFILTERED `count(*)`, and this is the third instance.** `occurrence_generation_test.sql`'s two closure assertions counted every class in the Prague studio on `current_date + 20` rather than the series under test — and the suite's own comment twenty lines above says why that is wrong and scopes the earlier assertions. They passed for as long as that date happened to be clear; run on 2026-09-11 it lands on a Thursday where an earlier section had already left a cancelled one-off and a `Clasher` class, so a count expecting 1 got 2. **Pre-existing, and reproduced with migration 102 removed entirely before anything was changed.** Now scoped to the series, and teeth-checked: resurrecting exactly that class makes it fail 1 → 0. After `scheduling_test.sql`'s notifications and `onboarding_test.sql`'s invites, this is the third — and the first where the count was polluted by the suite's own earlier sections rather than by another suite.

**Five teeth checks on the seat-cap suite, each run directly and each producing a real failure**: the studio switch (studio TWO starts refusing at its second sale), the cap comparison, `frozen` in the counted statuses, the `plan_seats()` guard, and the `false` in the Stripe handler. A teeth check that silently passes is worse than not running one, so each was required to stop the suite printing "all assertions passed" and its failing line was read.

**There is no automated guard on any of the seat-cap rendering.** The SQL half is 60 assertions; the switch hiding the plan form's Places section, the dependent fields appearing only once a limit is typed, the over-capacity warning, the refusal as a sentence, the renewal line for somebody already on a full plan, and the contrast were all verified by driving the browser and reading the DOM.

**THE ALLOWANCE MIRRORS THE CREDIT, AND THE SEAM'S OWN COMMENT WAS WRONG ABOUT THAT.** `tg_stamp_booking_release()` has said "WHEN PEAK ALLOWANCE ARRIVES, IT GOES HERE, **for 'studio_released' only**" since migration 079. Restoring on studio release alone would make the allowance two peak BOOKINGS a week rather than two peak CLASSES — a member who books and then cancels three days out has burned a slot, and members would learn not to book until the last minute, which is the opposite of what a studio wants from a schedule. A credit comes back on a timely cancellation and is taken on a late one; the whole reason the allowance exists is that an unlimited plan has no credit to take, so it has to behave like the thing it stands in for. `member_cancelled` and `studio_released` restore, `late_cancelled` does not, and `cancellation_cutoff_minutes` is still the only deadline. **The comment is corrected in 106 rather than left to mislead the next reader**, and it was wrong about the place as well as the rule: that is a BEFORE trigger, and a BEFORE trigger that writes to another table has written it even when a later one cancels the update. The consequence lives in an AFTER trigger beside the notification and timeline ones.

**THERE IS NO MIGRATION 105, and that is a finding rather than an omission.** The plan said `booking_release_reason` would need `flex_not_running` and `staff_excused`, carried alone because a new enum value cannot be used in the transaction that adds it. Neither is needed. **Flex-not-running is already `studio_released`** — `cancel_occurrence()` sets the `studiior.releasing` flag, and it SHOULD, because `release_reason` is what the studio owes the MEMBER and a class cancelled for want of one booking owes the member identically to one cancelled by a brownout; what differs is what it owes the INSTRUCTOR, and that is `cancellation_cause = 'unmet_minimum'` on the occurrence. This is the exact case those two typed facts were separated for. **Staff-excused is not a reason a booking was RELEASED** — a late cancellation staff later excuse was still released because the member cancelled late, and the excuse belongs on the ledger row that reverses the consequence. The three existing values are exactly sufficient.

**THE PEAK RULE IS AFTER §2.2, NOT IN THE §2.1 GATE, and that placement is the correctness argument.** The plan that PAYS is the plan held to its allowance. Resolving it up in the gate would mean a second plan-priority query beside the one rules 2 and 7 use, and the two would pick the same plan right until they did not — a member holding both an unlimited plan and a pack would have the pack's booking measured against the unlimited plan's allowance.

**"CONSUMED AT PROMOTION, NEVER AT JOIN" NEEDED NO CODE.** `respond_to_offer()` cancels the waitlisted row and calls `book_class()` again — so a promotion goes through the whole gate and is measured against the allowance as it stands at that moment. The trigger consumes on a seat becoming real and a `waitlisted` row is not one; cancelling that queue position refunds nothing, because the restore only writes where a consumption row exists. Without that correlation a waitlist cancellation would hand out an allowance nobody spent, which is what the suite asserts.

**`pending_payment` CONSUMES NOTHING**, for the same reason it has never counted toward the daily or forward limits: three abandoned checkouts must not exhaust a week's peak allowance on classes nobody paid for.

**A HOLLOW ASSERTION OF MINE, CAUGHT BY ITS OWN TEETH CHECK.** "A second cancellation of the same booking refunds nothing more" was written as a test of the `old.status is distinct from new.status` guard and passed with that guard deleted — because the re-cancel never reaches the insert, and if it did the unique index would refuse it. It now asserts the OUTCOME, which is what must hold however many mechanisms hold it, with the unique index asserted separately by attempting a duplicate row directly. **Two of the eight teeth checks on this work did not bite on the first attempt** and both were my fault: one sabotage changed a status test while the actual guard was a reason list, and this one tested a mechanism the path never reached.

**`daily_booking_cap` WAS ALREADY BUILT.** The brief asks for a per-plan daily cap; `membership_plans.max_bookings_per_day` has been that column since migration 001 and `book_class()` rule 2.1.7 has enforced it per plan in studio-local days since migration 002, with its own `daily_limit_reached` reason and a control on the plan form. Unlimited Monthly's "daily cap 1" needs no new code at all.

**THE MEMBER-FACING HALF, AND THE ONE RULE THAT SHAPES ALL OF IT: AN EXHAUSTED SLOT STAYS VISIBLE.** Hiding a peak class a member cannot book would make the timetable look emptier than it is and teach them the studio has nothing on at the hour they want. The class stays in its place, badged **Peak**, still showing "9 left", with the action replaced by the reason — *"No peak classes left this period"*. Verified in the DOM at 375px.

**The allowance is said once and always, not discovered at the moment of refusal.** A line above the list reads *"1 of 2 peak classes left this period. Off-peak classes are unlimited."*, and when it runs out, *"You have used all 2 of your peak classes for this period… your peak classes come back on Monday 21 September."* It is absent entirely for a studio with no peak hours and for a member on a plan with no allowance — there is nothing true to say in either case.

**`period_end` is a DATE, and a date has no timezone.** It is parsed AS UTC and formatted IN UTC so it round-trips exactly; handing it to a formatter in the studio's zone renders the day before for every studio west of Greenwich, which is this project's most repeated date bug in a new place.

**The confirmation is on the LAST one and nowhere else.** A dialog on every booking is a click everybody learns to dismiss; one that appears only when something is about to run out is one people read. Driven and verified both ways: with one left, `window.confirm` is called with *"This is your last peak class for this period. Book it?"* and declining leaves the class unbooked; with two left, **nothing is asked at all** and the booking goes straight through.

**The peak-window editor makes the number the point.** A studio drawing 07:00–09:00 on a grid cannot tell whether that is four classes a week or forty, and a window that catches the whole timetable is an allowance that stops everybody booking anything. Each window carries its own count of real upcoming classes and the panel totals them — measured on Reform Collective: *"24 of the next four weeks' classes fall in a peak window"*, with Monday's two windows showing 4 each and Tuesday's showing 0. Adding a window offers **every day** as one press, for the same reason the availability editor has copy-to-days: a studio's rush hours are the same all week and typing them seven times is how somebody decides not to bother.

**The plan form refuses to offer a field the CHECK would reject.** A peak allowance only appears on an unlimited recurring plan; on a pack or an 8-a-month plan the section says why instead — *"that number is its limit — a missed class costs a class, so a second penalty on top would charge twice for it"*. The server discards a posted allowance on any other plan type rather than trusting the form.

**Contrast measured in the rendered DOM for every new screen**: the peak settings panel at 1440 (99 elements, 0 below 4.5, lowest 4.59) and the member book page at 375 in both states (44 elements, 0 below, lowest 4.54). The **Peak** badge takes `--lime-text`, the accent's TEXT step, never its fill — a mark sitting ON a card is measured against the card, where the raw accent can be as low as 1.23.

**A live check that turned out to be the product working.** Booking three peak classes as a real member first returned `daily_limit_reached` three times — because `max_bookings_per_day = 1` had been set on the plan and the member already had seeded bookings on those days. That is rule 2.1.7, which has existed since migration 002, doing exactly the job the brief asked `daily_booking_cap` to do.

**A config change never reclassifies history, and the seed proves it.** Reform Collective's existing bookings on classes that are NOW peak consumed nothing, because the windows were drawn after those bookings were made. The ledger records what happened after the feature was turned on and nothing before it.

**A SUSPENSION IS DERIVED, NEVER STORED, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE DESIGN.** The brief names one edge as the most likely to be got wrong: marking a member present after a no-show has to void the infraction AND recalculate any suspension it triggered. Every version of that sentence which STORES a suspension needs a recalculation step, and a recalculation step forgotten in one of the four places an infraction can be voided is a member locked out for a fortnight over a class they attended. So nothing stores it — `member_suspension()` reads the live set of active infractions in the rolling window and computes the answer every time. Voiding an infraction recalculates nothing; it removes a row from a set, and the next read is already right. Same reason `ENDED IS DERIVED` on a series and the action centre has no `tasks` table.

**NOTHING IN THIS PRODUCT HAD EVER WRITTEN A NO-SHOW.** `booking_status` has carried `no_show` since migration 001, and `member_health`, `dashboard_kpis`, the roster and the pay ladder all READ it — but the only writers anywhere were `generate_demo_data()` and the seed. A class ran, and whoever did not come stayed `booked` for ever. So half of "infractions are late cancels AND no-shows" would have been a branch that could never fire, in the one place where it decides whether a real member is locked out. `sweep_no_shows()` gives it a writer and is **gated on this feature's own switch**: a studio that has not turned suspension on gets no marking and behaves exactly as today. That gate is a trade — the health score and the dashboard then see no-shows only at studios using suspension — and it is the narrower change. Marking every studio's absentees is a decision about member records, not about Decision 24.

**A SUSPENSION RESTRICTS ADVANCE BOOKING AND NOTHING ELSE.** Same-day still works, on whatever seats are left. A suspension that stopped somebody walking in would cost the studio the sale as well as the member the class, and it is harsher than any studio described wanting. The allowance still applies on top — a suspension does not hand out free peak slots. Both halves are asserted, and removing the date test makes the same-day assertion fail.

**THE BRIEF WAS WRONG ABOUT MARKING SOMEBODY PRESENT, AND THE REASON IS WHERE THE ALLOWANCE IS SPENT.** It says the correction restores the allowance. It does not: the slot is consumed at BOOKING, so a member who booked a peak class and was in the room has correctly spent it. Giving it back would hand a free peak class to everybody the desk corrected, and would mean a member is better off being marked absent and put right than being checked in properly. The infraction is voided — there was no infraction, the record was wrong — and nothing else changes.

**AN EXCUSED INFRACTION IS VOIDED, NEVER DELETED, AND THE EXCUSE RATE IS WHY.** A desk excusing most of them is telling a studio its own threshold is one nobody can defend at the counter, and deleting the rows makes that number impossible. `peak_allowance_report()` returns the rate and, past half, says what it means in words. An excuse with no note is refused: the rate is only worth measuring if each one can be read.

**THE REPORT STATES BOTH READINGS OF ITS OWN MAIN NUMBER.** Exhaustion is the figure a studio will act on and a percentage cannot express it alone — very high means the cap is too tight and is turning people away from classes with seats in them; near zero means it is not binding at all. The `reading` comes from SQL beside the number, so a threshold is not re-decided in a component.

**THE FREE-WINDOW REMINDER IS THE ONLY MESSAGE IN THIS PRODUCT THAT CHANGES WHAT HAPPENS.** Every other one reports something already done. At the moment this is sent, cancelling costs the member nothing and gives the studio a seat it can still fill; an hour later it costs them a peak slot and the studio has an empty reformer. Sent **only where it is true** — a booking that actually spent an allowance, at a studio with peak hours on, before a cutoff that has not passed — and keyed on the booking, so a fifteen-minute sweep sends it once.

**Three things about the template were checked against an existing one rather than assumed**: `html_body` is NOT NULL, the placeholder convention is single braces (`{first_name}`), and `render_notification()` substitutes by key. The first draft had double braces and no HTML body and failed at apply.

**A corrupted `.next` made a working page look broken.** The member screen rendered no infraction section while the data was plainly there and readable as the owner's own session — `Cannot find module './vendor-chunks/@supabase.js'`, because a `next build` had been run while the dev server was up and overwrote its cache. Worth knowing before diagnosing a render as a data problem: check `preview_logs` first.

**Eight more teeth checks, and one of mine was a no-op.** The sabotage for "excusing voids rather than deletes" was `booking_id = booking_id`, which changes nothing and proved nothing; redone as an actual DELETE, the assertion fails correctly. That is the second time this session a teeth check reported nothing because the sabotage was wrong rather than because the assertion was hollow — **a teeth check that does not bite is a claim about the SABOTAGE until you have read it.**

**THE SETTINGS AUDIT CAUGHT ME SHIPPING A FEATURE WITH NO WAY TO TURN IT ON.** `scripts/audit-settings-ui.py`, run after migration 109 as this file's own rule requires, reported **six new columns with no UI** — the whole suspension ladder and the reminder's lead time — and `suspension_enabled` itself had only a read in a page's select and no control anywhere. A studio could not have switched suspension on at all. That is `occurrence_horizon_days` again, in a feature built in the same session as the paragraph warning about it, and only the script caught it. Zero unreachable Decision 24 settings now.

**A CORRUPTED `.next` MADE TWO WORKING PANELS LOOK BROKEN, and the control experiment is what proved it.** The suspension switch would not reveal its numbers when clicked. Before touching the component, the same probe was run against `GuaranteesPanel` — shipped, unchanged, known to work — and it behaved identically: the checkbox ticked and its numbers stayed hidden. The page was serving no CSS and never hydrating, because a `next build` had been run while the dev server was up and overwrote its cache. `rm -rf .next` and a restart, and the tick reveals all five knobs. **When a UI probe fails, run it against something known to work before believing the new code is at fault.**

**THE DRIFT SCRIPT CRIED WOLF, AND THAT IS THE WORST THING IT CAN DO.** It reported `t_late_cancel(p_n integer, p_days integer)` as MISSING ON HOSTED. It is a helper defined in `test/peak_allowance_test.sql` — in no migration, referenced by no app code — so a local database that has run the suites carries it and hosted never will. The script's skip list was a hand-typed alternation (`expect_*`, `login`, `sig`, `psig`) that had been right until a suite added a helper outside that shape.

**A false positive here is expensive out of all proportion.** This tool has caught three real divergences and each was believed because its output is normally silent; one spurious alarm teaches you to skim the next one. **The list is now derived from `test/*.sql` themselves** — any helper any suite defines is excluded automatically, and nothing else is, so it cannot go stale the next time somebody adds one.

**And it cannot hide real drift.** A name is skipped only if a suite defines it AND no migration does. A function defined in both — a suite shadowing product code, itself worth knowing about — is compared as normal and named on stderr. Proved against a fixture where a suite redefines `book_class`: it is reported rather than skipped, while the two genuine helpers beside it are skipped. `--self-test` still passes and the real run is **IN STEP across 292 functions**.

**THE TIER READS AT A GLANCE ON ALL THREE SCREENS, AND IT IS A SHAPE RATHER THAN A COLOUR.** ● core, ○ flex, ◆ always, before the name so a column of them scans. Colour could not carry it: on the series grid the colour is the studio's own `class_types.color`, and **on the schedule calendar colour is already STAFFING** — amber for nobody teaching, coral and hatching for nobody teaching a class people have booked. The dashed edge could not carry it either, because that already means "flex, still waiting on its deadline", which is TEMPORARY and clears when the class commits; the tier is permanent, and conflating them is precisely why a committed flex class had until now looked exactly like a core one. No opacity anywhere near it.

**Measured in the rendered DOM on all three:** series list 59 elements 0 below 4.5 lowest 4.80, series grid 64/0/4.80 with the 13 marks at 6.08–7.04 on the class-type tints, schedule calendar 119/0/4.80 with 15 marks worst 7.11. Shown only when `guarantees_enabled OR flex_enabled` — **an OR, not an AND, and that is not hypothetical**: Reform Collective runs flex on and guarantees off, so an AND would have hidden the whole thing on the one studio it was built to be read against. With both off: zero marks on every screen, no legend sentence, and the row meta reverts to the rule alone.

**THE CALENDAR AND THE SERIES LIST DISAGREED, AND THE DATABASE WAS RIGHT.** `schedule_range()` has returned `occ_tier` since migration 095, resolved through `occurrence_guarantee()` — which demotes a tier whose switch is off to `'always'`, correctly, because with core evaluation off a core class genuinely does run regardless. But that demotion is studio-wide and carries no per-class information, so the calendar marked every one of Reform Collective's six core series "always runs" while the list beside it said "core". Migration 110 returns BOTH: `occ_tier` unchanged and still what the booking engine will do, `occ_series_tier` what the studio set. The MARK shows what it is; the demotion is what the mark says on hover.

**`class_series.guarantee_tier` IS NOT NULL WITH A DEFAULT OF 'core', WHICH MAKES THE OBVIOUS MIRROR WRONG.** A TypeScript `coalesce(column, boolean, 'core')` never reaches the boolean, so it reports core for every flex series created with Decision 21's writer — and **there are still two writers**: `set_series_flex()` sets `flex` and `minimum_bookings` and never touches the column, `set_series_guarantee()` sets the column. The flex suite's own fixtures are the first shape, and so is any studio that ran Decision 21 before Decision 22 landed. What saves those rows in the database is that `set_series_flex()` propagates the boolean DOWN to the occurrences, where the engine meets it at the second arm of the walk. So the honest question for a series row is not what the coalesce says but what its classes will be, and the answer is that a true boolean means flex whatever the column was left at. Caught by driving a legacy-shaped row, not by reading.

**A PRE-EXISTING CONTRAST FAILURE, FOUND BECAUSE THE INSTRUMENT WAS WRONG FIRST.** The probe parsed `rgb()` only, and `color-mix()` resolves to `color(srgb …)` — so every measurement over the series grid's tinted blocks was nonsense (ratios in the billions) and I nearly reported them as passing. With a parser that reads both, the calendar's **"On its own" measured 3.58** against a 4.5 floor: `--amber-deep` setting a sentence, which the palette does not allow — it is derived as a DOT colour at 3:1. It never appeared locally because no seeded studio had a flex series to be standalone. It takes `--ink-2` now. **And the quiet blocks carried `opacity: 0.92` on their own labels** — the thing this project has undone twice — buying nothing, since quiet is already carried by the plain surface, the grey border and the word. Removed, which is also what makes the measurement exact rather than approximately right.

**`object-fit: cover` KEPT SEVENTEEN PER CENT OF THE LOGIN PHOTOGRAPH, AND THE ARITHMETIC IS WORTH WRITING DOWN.** A 1600×600 picture on a 375×812 phone is scaled to fill the height — 812/600 = 1.35× — which makes it 2165px wide, and 375 of those survive. Measured with an eight-column test image: the sign-in screen kept **columns 4 and 5** and nothing else, and a subject at 22% of the frame was not on screen at all. `contain` is not the fix; it leaves empty bands top and bottom on a portrait screen, which looks worse than a crop.

**A STUDIO'S SHOT OF ITS REFORMERS IS RARELY CENTRED, so the fix is to crop around something else.** `object-position` from two bounded ints — ints rather than a text column because the value lands in an inline style, and a string from the database in a style attribute is a surface that does not need to exist. Bounded 0–100 by a CHECK and clamped again in `lib/focal.ts`.

**THE PREVIEW SHAPE WAS THE WHOLE REASON NOBODY SAW IT.** The branding screen showed the login photo in a 28px-tall full-width band — landscape, the one shape that always looks fine. It previews at **375×812** now, measured in the DOM at ratio 0.462, and that preview IS the picker: click it, drag it, or use the arrow keys. The class-type picker previews at the member app's real card shape, 343×172.

**FIVE RENDER SITES, AND I MISSED ONE ON THE FIRST PASS.** The login screen, the member home's hero, the "Coming up" cards and the class detail screen. The coming-up card is 124×88 and uses different markup — no `absolute inset-0` — so a pattern-matched edit skipped it, and only grepping every `object-cover` in `app/member` afterwards found it. Verified in the DOM: hero at ratio 1.99 and three cards at 1.41, all carrying the same point.

**AVATARS CROP THE SAME WAY AND AN ORDER OF MAGNITUDE LESS, which is why they are left alone and this says so.** Every avatar frame is SQUARE — `rounded-full` at a fixed size — and for a square target a 3:4 portrait keeps 75% of its height and a 16:9 landscape keeps 56% of its width, against the login screen's 17%. A face is also usually near the centre of the axis that gets cropped. If it ever does need fixing, `instructors.avatar_url` is the one to do first: the studio uploads those and there is already a form to hang the control on, where member avatars are uploaded by members and would need the picker inside the member app.

**RUNNING `next build` WHILE THE DEV SERVER IS UP OVERWRITES ITS CACHE, and it has now cost time twice in one session.** The symptom is a page that renders blank or unstyled and never hydrates — `Cannot find module './vendor-chunks/@supabase.js'` in `preview_logs` — and it looks exactly like a broken component. `rm -rf .next` and restart. Check `preview_logs` before diagnosing a render as a data problem.

**A MONTH IS A DRAFT UNTIL PUBLISHED, AND "PUBLISHED" IS ONE PREDICATE READ BY EVERYTHING.** Decision 25. `month_published(studio, at)` answers the two RLS policies on `class_occurrences`, `book_class()`, both `instructor_week()`s, `confirm_week()`, `unconfirmed_summary()`, the week-confirmation ask, `instructor_roster()`, `commitment_pending()` and `queue_instructor_assigned()`. It is true for every studio with the switch off and for any month before the studio's current one — history is published by definition, or turning the switch on would blank an instructor's past weeks. The predicate is studio-local: a 23:30 UTC class on 30 September is October in Manila. There is no state on the occurrence and nothing to unpublish, because there is no writer for it.

**PUBLICATION REFUSES BEFORE THE WINDOW, and the order is the correctness argument.** An unpublished class is invisible to a member, so a member reaching it by id is refused for the reason it is invisible — `month_not_published`, about the CLASS — and `outside_booking_window` keeps meaning what it has since migration 002: a class on the timetable this member may not book YET, about the MEMBER. When both apply, publication first; once the month is out, the window is the only true reason left, and the suite asserts both halves on one class.

**TURNING THE SWITCH ON PUBLISHES WHAT IT MAY NOT HIDE.** The current month has already started, and a future month members have booked into cannot be withdrawn — so `set_publication_enabled()` publishes both automatically, marked `auto`, emailing nobody, and says which. A plain UPDATE of the column would have been "unpublish" arriving by the back door.

**THE ROSTER EMAIL LISTS THE CLASSES IN THE BODY.** An instructor reads it on a phone between two classes and decides whether to say yes; sending them a link to look it up is sending them nothing. The link is where they say yes — and it is a FULL URL. **Migration 067's two week-confirmation emails had carried `/my/week?w=…` as a path since they were written**, which in an email is a link to nowhere; fixed in 112 while the sweep was re-issued.

**`move_occurrence()` REFUSED IN WORDS AND WROTE ANYWAY.** Its availability-dates check sat after the UPDATE and returned rather than raised, so `{"ok": false, "reason": "outside_availability_dates"}` came back with the class already reassigned. Proved on the seed before touching it — instructor 41's window ended yesterday, the move was refused, the class was 41's — and moved ahead of the write in 112, with the suite asserting the row is unchanged. Live since 061.

**A FIXTURE THAT COMPUTES "TODAY" FROM `current_date` IS WRONG FOR TWO HOURS A NIGHT.** `instructor_self_service_test.sql` set a Prague studio's ask day from the server's UTC date, and at 22:31 UTC — 00:31 Saturday in Prague — set it to Friday and failed. Every "today" in that suite now comes from `(now() at time zone 'Europe/Prague')::date`. The same trap as `starts_on` in migration 095, in a test rather than a function.

**A BLANK TEETH RESULT IS NOT A PASS.** Two sabotages printed nothing because the sabotage had not applied — `psql -f` exits 0 on an error without `ON_ERROR_STOP`, and a substring match on `  end if;` had removed an inner `end if` and left a syntax error. Two more printed nothing because the assertion was hollow: confirming a draft month raises PT409 and so does confirming an empty one, so an assertion on the code alone passed with the publication check deleted; and studio C's unpublished month sat outside the action centre's fortnight, so the switch was never the only thing keeping the row out. All four were read rather than counted, and the two hollow ones now assert the message and widen the window. **Fourteen teeth checks on Decision 25, every one read.**

**THE SUCCESS MESSAGE LANDED ON THE WRONG MONTH.** The publish screen selected the first draft on every render, so publishing October re-selected November and the "October is published" line sat above a "Publish November" button. The action now redirects to the month it published and the outcome is rendered from the facts — who was sent a roster, who could not be — rather than from a message carried back.

**Twenty open cover forms is a wall.** The instructor's month screen first rendered the full ask-for-cover form under every class; it is a native `<details>` now, one line per class, opening on demand, with no state to lose.

**THE FOUR SHIFT/FLEX SURFACES ARE WIRED.** open_shift() has a control on the roster (`/roster/[occurrenceId]`, which the calendar links to): a disclosure that takes the named instructor off with a required reason, manager-up, only on a live class with an instructor. The reliability record (115) shows as plain context beside each applicant on `/shifts/applications` — "applied for 2, withdrew from 1 · 1 at short notice" — and on the instructor's own `/instructor/me`, said as "nobody is scored on this". The portal's "withdraw application" now calls `withdraw_application()` rather than `withdraw_from_shift()`, which had 403'd on an open shift since 064. A not-running flex class draws on the staff calendar as a muted, struck-through block reading "Not running — N booked, needed M" (NOT the dashed flex-waiting edge, which is a different state), and is filtered out of the member `/book` and home lists with an explicit `status = 'scheduled'` — belt to the RLS braces, closing the own-read path for a member who had booked it. Part 2's alert is email-only (`notify_open_shifts` cron); there is no screen.

**CHALLENGES ARE BUILT END TO END, members only (§9).** Staff: `/challenges` (list, gated in the rail on the studio having one — no trace otherwise), `/challenges/new` (create from a seeded system template or blank; the date rules live in `create_challenge`, not a bare insert), `/challenges/[id]` (participants, progress, ranks, and a Publish control on a draft). Members: **no sixth tab** — a Challenges section on Home (only when the studio runs them), `/challenges` (open + joined with a progress bar), `/challenges/[id]` (their own progress always, the leaderboard only when the studio turned it on, and the classes counted so far). Joining is optimistic and backfills from the start. The dashboard shows the Challenge Participation KPI once a challenge exists. Verified in the browser on the seed: the rail item appears, staff create redirects to a draft overview, a member joins and backfills to 2/8, the themed leaderboard ranks them #1 with their own row highlighted, the Home section shows 2/8, and the KPI reads 7. `lib/database.types.ts` was regenerated for the new RPCs. There is no JS test runner, so the screens are driven-and-shown; the §9 mechanics are the 29 SQL assertions and two teeth checks.

**Next:** selling a plan to a member (§9 gives front desk that, unlike editing), then cancellation and the waitlist promotion flow.

---

## Rules that are not negotiable

**RLS decides which rows, never which columns.** `members_self_update` is `using (user_id = auth.uid())` and `authenticated` holds UPDATE on all 28 columns of `members`, so "a member may edit their own row" meant a member could set `status`, `waiver_signed_at`, `health_band`, `lifetime_visits` and `studio_id`. Proved: the seeded member with no waiver signed her own and booked the class the gate had just refused.

Column grants cannot fix it — front desk, managers and members are all `authenticated`, so narrowing the grant takes the same columns from the staff who are meant to edit them. The rule lives in a trigger (migration 035) that compares `to_jsonb(new) - owned` against `to_jsonb(old) - owned`, so **a column added later is protected the day it is created** rather than the day somebody remembers it. Any table with a self-service write path needs the same shape.

**A `guard_member_self_update` flag exemption is COLUMN-NARROWED, never a blanket `return new`.** A SECURITY DEFINER writer that must set an otherwise-protected member column as the member — `sign_waiver_document` setting `waiver_signed_at`, the self check-in health cascade setting `health_*` — raises a transaction-local flag the guard honours. As of migration 170 **both** flags are narrow: `studiior.waiver_signing` exempts only `waiver_signed_at` (+`updated_at`); `studiior.checkin_health` only `health_band`/`health_reason`/`health_signals`/`health_computed_at` (+`updated_at`). The branch checks `(to_jsonb(new) - <flag's columns>) <> (to_jsonb(old) - <flag's columns>)` and raises PT403 if anything OUTSIDE the writer's own columns changed — so even if a flag ever leaked to a member session (a client cannot `set_config`, but the exemption must not depend on that alone), it can still write only that writer's columns, never the rest of the row. **ANY NEW FLAG ADDED HERE MUST BE COLUMN-NARROWED THE SAME WAY** — list exactly the columns its writer touches and refuse the rest; a bare `return new` is the WIDE form and is the bug. Each flag needs a teeth test (a non-allowed write under the flag → PT403) with a teeth-of-teeth (the WIDE form makes that teeth fail) — proven for both flags in `self_checkin_test`/`waiver_test`, and confirmed on hosted (a member session holding `checkin_health` is refused `update members set email=…` PT403).

**RLS is the security boundary.** A permission that exists only in React is not a permission. Every rule in the permissions doc is a policy. Instructor access to revenue is denied at the policy level, not by hiding menu items.

**Every table gets RLS and a grant.** RLS decides which rows; grants decide whether the role may touch the table at all. Both, or the table is either closed to everyone or open to everyone. See migration 001 §16 — but read migration 005 with it: §16 claims there is no pre-login surface, and since migration 004 there is exactly one, `studio_by_slug()`.

**Functions are closed by default, because PostgreSQL's default is the opposite.** A new function is executable the moment it is created. Withholding a grant does nothing; you have to revoke. Say who may execute, every time.

**Revoking from `PUBLIC` is not the same as revoking from `anon`, and only hosted can tell you.** The hosted platform ships default privileges naming `anon` explicitly, so every function created by `postgres` in `public` is born with an `anon=X` grant. The local stack does not do this. Migration 006 revoked from `PUBLIC`, passed every local check, and left eight functions anon-callable in production for five migrations; 011 revoked from `anon` and flipped the hosted default so the next one is not born open.

The lesson is not about one grant. **`supabase db reset` cannot verify grants** — local and hosted have different default ACLs, so the tests agree with the wrong answer. After any migration that creates a function or touches privileges, run the advisor query against the hosted project, not just locally:

```bash
supabase db query --linked "select p.proname, has_function_privilege('anon',p.oid,'execute') as anon, has_function_privilege('authenticated',p.oid,'execute') as authed from pg_proc p join pg_namespace n on n.oid=p.pronamespace where n.nspname='public' and (has_function_privilege('anon',p.oid,'execute') or has_function_privilege('authenticated',p.oid,'execute')) order by 1;"
```

**Ask about `authenticated` too, not only `anon`.** Migration 033 exists because this query used to ask about `anon` alone: `anon` is the role we remember to fear, and `authenticated` is every member of every studio, including a walk-in who signed up thirty seconds ago. A function with no guard inside it is as exposed to one as to the other.

The only names of ours that belong in that output are the **eleven** pre-login surfaces, and these are the exact signatures hosted returns: `studio_by_slug(text)`, `studio_invite_preview(text)`, `accept_studio_invite(text,text,text)`, `member_invite_preview(text)`, `claim_member_account(text,text,text)`, `instructor_invite_preview(text)`, `claim_instructor_account(text,text,text)`, `stripe_webhook(text,text)`, `stripe_platform_webhook(text,text)`, `public_schedule(text,int)` and `calendar_feed(text)`. **The tenth is `public_schedule` (migration 147, the public timetable embed): anon AND authenticated, a READ, read-through cached.** **The eleventh is `calendar_feed` (migration 162, Decision 33 Part B, the subscribable calendar feed): anon AND authenticated, a READ, read-through cached, resolving a HASHED token to a person's own future classes — an unknown or revoked token returns null and writes nothing.** The two stripe webhooks WRITE tenant data with no session (the signature is the credential); `public_schedule` and `calendar_feed` write only their OWN cache row, `public_schedule` after resolving a real active studio by slug (an unknown slug returns `{found:false}`) and `calendar_feed` after resolving a live token (the token is the credential) — see the rule below. **The count has been exactly ten since 147 and is exactly eleven since 162 — verified on hosted after pushing 162: `calendar_feed` anon=true, and no twelfth.** **Nine, not seven: migration 097's instructor pair are pre-login by nature, and this paragraph said seven for as long as that was untrue while the paragraph forty lines above said nine.** A file that disagrees with itself about a security surface is worse than one that says nothing, and the count has now been wrong in this file three times — "three" when it was three and four more were added, "seven" when 097 made it nine, and `claim_member_account` carried a two-argument signature here that has never existed. **Verified against hosted after migration 102: exactly these nine, no extras, none absent.** The earlier scare is still worth keeping — the query returned **two wrong extras** at migration 085, `set_series_flex` and `set_occurrence_guaranteed` from 075, both anon-callable on hosted and closed by 086. Neither was reachable through, since `is_manager_up()` is false for a caller with no session, but an anon caller could still tell an id that exists (PT403) from one that does not (PT404). Only hosted could say so.

Note what migration 013 found: the query has to enumerate what is *actually there* on hosted, not what this repo creates. `rls_auto_enable()` is installed by the platform, exists on no local stack, and sat anon-callable through migration 011 because 011 only checked its own list.

**Platform billing is not Connect and shares nothing with it.** Connect is the studio's members paying the studio on the studio's account; `platform_subscriptions` is the studio paying us on ours. Two endpoints, two signing secrets, and each refuses the other's events — the platform one rejects anything carrying an `account`, the Connect one anything without. Applying a member's class payment to our own subscription table is the failure this prevents.

**There is no manual fallback for platform billing, and the screen says so.** A studio may take cash from its own members forever under Decision 16; it still pays us by card. We are not chasing bank transfers from ten studios, and a dunning process for our own invoices is not the product. Said plainly in `app/staff/billing` rather than left to be discovered.

**Lockout is a status, never a deletion.** Every read stays open — rosters, members, history, the schedule — so a locked studio never looks like lost data, and paying flips one column back. `cancel_booking()` is deliberately ungated: a studio that cannot cancel a class leaves members at a locked door on a Tuesday, and those members did nothing wrong. Cancelling matters *more* when a studio is in trouble.

**A member of a locked studio is told nothing about why.** `/unavailable` says the studio is not taking bookings and that their history is intact. Not that a subscription lapsed, not what it costs — their studio's business with us is not theirs, and "your studio didn't pay" tells a member something about their instructor's finances they were never entitled to.

**The lockout gate is in `staffScreen()` and `memberScreen()`, not middleware.** Middleware would have to resolve the signed-in user's studio on every request to know which subscription to check; these two helpers are what every screen already goes through with the studio id in hand. They are the real chokepoint. The database gates in migration 045 are the actual boundary either way — a permission that exists only in the app is not one.

**Warnings count down rather than repeat.** Banner from day one of grace showing days remaining, and email on days 1, 7, 12 and 14, deduped per studio per grace period per day. The day is computed by ROUNDING the remaining interval: `extract(day from ...)` truncates 13.4 days to 13, and day one would never have fired at all.

**Studiior is a booking platform, not a payment processor — Decision 16.** A studio records payments however it already takes money: cash, bank transfer, GCash, a terminal on the counter. An online provider is an optional adapter on top. Stripe does not serve the Philippines at all, and a Stripe-shaped product would have excluded design partners whose only problem is booking.

**One path, not two that match.** What makes a manual payment activate a membership *identically* to a Stripe one is that both call `activate_purchase()` and `confirm_dropin_payment()` — the only code that grants anything. Two implementations would agree the day they were written and drift after. The test proves it by diffing the two membership rows field for field.

**A RECURRING MEMBERSHIP SOLD AS CASH HAD NO BILLING PERIOD, AND NOTHING COULD SEE IT.** `activate_purchase()` wrote status, price, credits and `auto_renew` and left `current_period_start`, `current_period_end` and `renews_on` null. A recurring membership with no period is one nothing can bill, renew or expire: §7.3's grace is measured from `current_period_end`, the member screen could not say when it renewed, and `book_class()`'s past-due allowance — `now() < coalesce(ms.current_period_end, now()) + grace` — collapses to `now() < now() + grace`, **true for ever**. Both screens already read `renews_on`; they had simply never had one to show.

**DECISION 16'S OWN TEST DID NOT CATCH IT, AND THAT IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING.** It *does* use a recurring plan and it compares the cash membership and the Stripe membership field for field. **Both were null.** The Stripe half is driven by a `checkout.session.completed`, whose object carries no `current_period_*` — those arrive in a later subscription event the test never sends. The assertion held because neither side did the thing. **Two implementations agreeing proves nothing when both are wrong**; it is "a guard that never fires looks exactly like a guard that passes" wearing an equality check. The comparison now has to be comparing something, and asserts both sides end on the same day.

**The period is computed in the studio's local time and converted back**, never by adding an interval to a timestamptz — a month added to an instant is added in UTC, so a 07:00 boundary in Prague becomes 06:00 or 08:00 across a clock change and the billing day drifts. Verified: a renewal across the October change keeps 21:43 Prague on both sides while the UTC instant moves an hour.

**`starts_on` and `expires_on` were the SERVER's day, and that was a live bug of its own.** They used `current_date`; both fixture studios in the manual-payments suite are in Manila, so for sixteen hours out of every twenty-four a pack sold in the morning expired ninety days from *yesterday* — one day short of what the member paid for. It read as correct because the assertion was written against the same wrong clock the function used.

**RENEWAL: RECORDING A PAYMENT ADVANCES THE PERIOD, IT DOES NOT SELL A SECOND MEMBERSHIP.** A cash studio has no webhook, so the desk taking next month's money IS the renewal. **It advances from `current_period_end`, never from today** — paying four days early must not shorten the next month and paying four days late must not move the anniversary for ever. A payment so late that one interval forward is still in the past leaves the membership past due and SAYS SO, which is correct: two months behind owes two months, and the desk records the second payment. Rolling to today would forgive the arrears and drift the billing day in one stroke. A different plan is a plan change and still sells a membership; a pack is never a renewal.

**One definition of "frozen right now", because the first version had two.** §7.4's freeze is a pair of DATES and `status = 'frozen'` is a separate fact a studio may not have set — `book_class()` has always read the dates. The renewal guard first read only the status and cheerfully renewed a frozen membership; `membership_frozen_now()` is what everything migration 095 adds now asks.

**`sweep_membership_periods()` is scoped to memberships with NO Stripe subscription**, which is the whole gap. A Connect subscription rolls its own period forward and says `past_due` through `invoice.payment_failed`; sweeping those here would mark a member overdue for the minutes between a successful renewal and its webhook arriving.

**A studio taking cash can finally see WHO OWES IT MONEY.** `/due`, front desk and up — §9 reads their "Payments" as taking payment and the desk is who chases one. Overdue first, with what they owe, how long it has been and a button that takes the money from that row. **What each owes is the membership's own `price_cents`, never the plan's**: §7.1 snapshots the price so an edit cannot reprice anybody already on it, and a chase list quoting today's price would undo that at the counter. Subscription-backed, frozen, cancelled and expired are all absent, and "no recurring plans at all" is a different state from "everybody is paid up" — a screen that cannot tell them apart says "nobody owes you anything" to a studio whose memberships are all on Stripe.

**A HOLLOW ASSERTION OF MY OWN, CAUGHT BY THE TEETH CHECK.** The test that proves the due list quotes the snapshot price raised the plan's price first — as the FRONT DESK session, where `plans_manager_write` is manager-up and a refused UPDATE does not raise, it changes nothing. The price never moved, and the assertion passed against both the right answer and the wrong one. It runs as postgres now, and asserts the price actually moved before asserting what the list says about it.

**Open, and not decided:** a member may hold two active recurring memberships on different plans, and both appear on the due list owing separately. Whether selling plan B should supersede plan A is a plan-change decision nobody has made.

**The tradeoff is recorded, not policed.** A manual payment is the studio's own bookkeeping: they reconcile it, and a membership can be marked paid when no money moved. Nothing tries to stop that. What the row owes them is who recorded it, when, by what method and against what reference — enough to reconcile, not enough to police. A booking platform that audits its customers' cash handling has misunderstood what it is for.

**Front desk takes money; only a manager gives it back.** Permissions §9 reads front desk "Payments" as *taking* payment. `record_manual_payment()` is `is_desk_up()`, `record_refund()` is `is_manager_up()` — money leaving the studio needs a second pair of hands.

**A full refund takes back what is left, never what was used.** Refunding a pack removes the unused credits and cancels the membership; it does not claw back classes already attended, because you cannot un-attend a Tuesday. A partial refund does not touch credits at all — what a half-refunded pack is worth is the studio's judgement, not a function's guess.

**An optional checklist item does not hold the list open.** `connect_stripe` is optional under Decision 16, so it is excluded from the count in `lib/setup.ts` as well as marked in the UI. A studio taking cash in Manila has finished setting up, and a checklist stuck at "6 of 7" forever teaches people to stop reading it.

**A webhook has no session, so the signature is the credential.** Every other write in this codebase goes through RLS with a real user behind it. Stripe has no user and RLS cannot express "Stripe said so", so the choice was a service-role key that can do anything to any tenant, or a function that will not act without a valid signature. `stripe_webhook()` recomputes the HMAC-SHA256 over the raw body in Postgres before it reads a single field — the same shape as `resolve_checkin_code()`, where the cryptography is the gate rather than the caller's identity. There is still no service-role client in this codebase.

The body must reach it as TEXT and unchanged: the HMAC is over the exact bytes Stripe sent, and a `JSON.parse` followed by a `JSON.stringify` reorders keys and invalidates it.

**The tenant comes from the `account` field, never from metadata.** Stripe puts the connected account id at the top level of a Connect event; we put metadata inside the object. An event whose metadata names a different studio than the account resolved to is refused outright, and an event for an account we do not know is recorded and NOT processed rather than guessed at.

**Idempotency is the insert, not a check.** `stripe_events` has Stripe's own event id as its primary key, so a replay conflicts, inserts nothing and returns before reaching a handler. Stripe retries on any non-2xx for days, so this is a certainty rather than a precaution — which is also why `duplicate` and `unknown_account` both answer 200: neither is something Stripe can fix by sending it again.

**A held seat is not a booking.** A drop-in at a Stripe-connected studio is created as `pending_payment`, not `booked`. It holds its seat — taking someone's place while they type a card number is worse than briefly overstating how full a class is — but it does not count toward the daily or forward limits, it sends no confirmation, and it reads as "holding your spot" with a way back to Checkout. Three abandoned checkouts must not use up three of today's classes.

It is a status and not a flag on purpose: thirty-four places filter on booking status, and with a flag every one of them would go on treating an unpaid booking as confirmed until somebody remembered otherwise. As a status the default falls the right way and the two places that DO need it have to say so.

**The sweep cancels through `cancel_booking()`, never by updating rows itself.** That is the only reason that function accepts a service context. Doing the update inline would be shorter and would skip §4.2, so the seat would come free and nobody on the waitlist would ever hear about it — worse than not freeing it. A seat that was only ever held is also never recorded as a *late* cancellation: no member should carry a black mark for a class they never paid for.

**How long the hold lasts is the studio's setting**, `dropin_payment_window_minutes`, beside the other timing rules. A 6am reformer class wants its seat back quickly and a quiet Sunday mat class does not.

**Deferrable is not a carve-out.** The instructor constraint is `DEFERRABLE INITIALLY IMMEDIATE`, so two instructors can swap two simultaneous classes inside one transaction — a valid end state that is refused statement by statement without it. The database still refuses to COMMIT a double-booking; it only tolerates the journey. The room constraint is left immediate as instructed, and has the same swap friction.

**`substitute_for` is historical; `instructor_id` is who teaches.** A class being subbed already stops counting against the original instructor, so no carve-out was needed for substitution — subbing somebody in is refused only when they genuinely cannot be in two places.

**A move that changes the arrangement owes a free cancellation.** More than `significant_move_hours`, or onto a different day in studio time, sets `bookings.free_cancel_until`, which `cancel_booking()` honours over the studio's cutoff. Decision 2's reasoning: they agreed to a time and the studio changed it, so charging them for cancelling is charging them for the studio's decision.

**Nothing moves on screen until the database has answered.** The calendar's first version applied the drag optimistically and reverted on refusal, which meant an accidental two-pixel drag visibly relocated a class with eight people in it before anyone was asked. The confirm now comes first, and it says how many members will be emailed.

**An undo withdraws the email rather than sending a second one.** Moving a class back within sixty seconds is read as a correction: the unsent `class_moved` rows are deleted and nothing new is queued. Any move also deletes the previous unsent notice for that class, because it describes a move that has been superseded.

**What cannot physically overlap is an exclusion constraint, not a check.** A room cannot hold two classes and a person cannot teach two at once. `occ_room_no_overlap` and `occ_instructor_no_overlap` are GiST exclusions — the first use of the `btree_gist` that migration 014 left installed — so they hold against every writer including an import, the seed and a hand-typed UPDATE. Both are partial: a cancelled class stops holding its room, and an open shift has no instructor to clash with but still holds its slot. The opclass is named explicitly (`extensions.gist_uuid_ops`) rather than resolved through search_path.

**Availability is a warning; overlap is a wall.** Decision 9's rule that a hard block gets worked around by not using the feature applies to availability, which is a judgement. It does not apply to two classes in one room, which is not.

**One function moves a class.** `move_occurrence()` backs the calendar's drag, its resize and the edit form. Before Decision 17 there was nothing to share — `createClass` was a bare insert with no conflict checking at all — so "the calendar must not bypass the form's rules" meant writing the rules, not reusing them. With members booked it refuses on the first call and returns the count; the caller confirms and the second call moves it and queues `class_moved`. A drag that silently emails forty people because a finger slipped is worse than one that asks.

**A derived column needs a trigger, not a default.** `class_occurrences.staffing` has a CHECK tying it to `instructor_id`, and no default can satisfy it — 'assigned' is wrong for a row inserted without an instructor and 'open' is wrong for one with. `tg_derive_staffing()` makes staffing follow `instructor_id` unless the caller already wrote a consistent pair. Without it the CHECK rejected ten existing suites and the seed, none of which know the column exists and none of which should have to.

**A library stylesheet imported from a component wins on source order.** react-big-calendar's CSS is imported inside the calendar component, which Next injects AFTER `globals.css` — so a bare `.rbc-today` override loses at equal specificity and the grid stayed the library's blue. Every override is scoped under `.rbc-calendar`, which wins on specificity rather than on order. Same lesson as the health chip's label, in reverse.

**Booking runs in one transaction with a row lock.** `select ... from class_occurrences where id = $1 for update` before reading `booked_count`. Application-level check-then-insert will overbook under load and is not acceptable.

**Money is integer cents plus an ISO currency code.** Never floats.

**A price is snapshotted, never referenced.** `memberships.price_cents` is copied from the plan at purchase (§7.1). Editing a plan must never reprice anyone already on it, and any screen that edits a plan has to say so, because "changed the price" reads like "changed what everyone pays" unless you tell people otherwise.

**A guard comparing a nullable column to `auth.uid()` uses `exists()` or `coalesce()`, NEVER a bare equality inside `if not (...)`.** This is the same NULL-deny gap as the helper rule above, one level out: `if not (m.user_id = auth.uid() or is_desk_up(...)) then raise` does NOT fire when `m.user_id` is NULL — every unclaimed guest, lead and import — because `null = auth.uid()` is NULL, `NULL or false` is NULL (the helpers return false, never null), and `if NULL then raise` is skipped, so any signed-in user can act on that member's row at any studio. Write it `coalesce(m.user_id = auth.uid(), false)` or `exists (select 1 from members m where m.id = ... and m.user_id = auth.uid())` — a WHERE-clause NULL just excludes the row, so `exists()` is null-safe and so is any comparison in a query. This is the SAME bug FOUR times: migration 020 (the helpers), 035 (`members_self_update`), 129 (`sign_waiver`), 130 (`cancel_booking`, `respond_to_offer`, `choose_pay_at_desk`). **`scripts/check-null-guards.py` sweeps for it** — every `if`-guard that compares to `auth.uid()` without `coalesce`/`exists` — and is the repeatable guard against the fifth; run it after any migration that adds or re-issues a SECURITY DEFINER function, the way `check-hosted-drift.sh` guards against edited-in-place migrations. It catches the `auth.uid()` ownership idiom specifically (a pure text check, no parsing); it does NOT catch an arbitrary nullable column compared to a non-`auth.uid()` value, which would need real parsing.

**A boolean authorisation helper must never return null.** `auth_role_in()` returns null for a caller who is staff of no studio, so `role in ('owner','manager')` is null, not false. RLS treats that as deny and is safe. plpgsql does not: `if not is_manager_up(x) then raise ... end if` skips its own raise, and the function carries on — and these functions are `SECURITY DEFINER`, so nothing is standing behind the guard. Every such guard in the codebase reads that way, and every one of them was open to any signed-in user who knew an id. Migration 020 fixes it in the helpers rather than at the call sites, because the call sites are the natural way to write it and the next one will be written the same way. When adding a helper, `coalesce(..., false)`.

**A guard that never fires looks exactly like a guard that passes.** The §5 tests for the importer used a front desk *of the same studio* — a real role, so the guard worked and the assertions passed. The caller who got through was the one with no staff row in that studio at all, which nothing exercised. When testing a permission, include a caller the check has never seen, not only a caller with the wrong role.

**A refused write does not raise — it changes nothing.** RLS blocks an INSERT with a WITH CHECK violation, which errors, but it blocks an UPDATE or DELETE by making the row invisible: PostgREST returns 200 and an empty array. Code that only checks `error` will report success having saved nothing. Check rows affected on every update and delete.

**A studio's timezone is validated in the database, not the form.** It governs every day boundary and every materialised occurrence, and a typo does not fail — `Europe/Pragu` is simply not a zone, and the studio's classes are quietly wrong from then on. `validate_iana_timezone()` (migration 015) checks against `pg_timezone_names` on insert and update, so the wizard, `provision_studio()` and any future import all meet the same rule. The UI offers `Intl.supportedValuesOf('timeZone')` with live UTC offsets so the value can only come from a list; that is convenience, not the guarantee.
